[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

    pat = []

def get_b_pattern(tile):
    """Extract the B pattern from a tile (positions where char is B)"""
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in row) for row in tile]

def compare_patterns(p1, p2):
    """Compare two 7x7 patterns, count matching B positions"""
    match = 0
    total = 0
    for r in range(7):
        for c in range(7):
            if p1[r][c]=='B' or p2[r][c]=='B':
                total += 1
                if p1[r][c]==p2[r][c]=='B':
                    match += 1
    return match, total

print("Top tile B patterns:")
for name, tile in top_tiles.items():
    bp = get_b_pattern(tile)
    color = tile[0][0]
    print(f"{name} ({color}):")
    for row in bp:
        print("  ", row)
    print()

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    """Rotate a 7x7 pattern 90 degrees clockwise"""
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# Unmatched S tiles
s_tiles = {
    'R1T1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 12, 18),
    'R1T3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 35, 41),
    'R2T3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 35, 41),
    'R3T1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 22, 28, 12, 18),
}

# Unmatched S subs
s_subs = {
    'S1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 14, 20),
    'S3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 28, 34),
    'S5': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 42, 48),
}

# Unmatched P tiles
p_tiles = {
    'R1T4': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 45, 51),
    'R2T4': extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 45, 51),
}

# Unmatched P subs
p_subs = {
    'P2': extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27),
}

print("S tiles vs S subs (checking all rotations):")
for tname, tpat in s_tiles.items():
    for sname, spat in s_subs.items():
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if compare(rpat, spat):
                print(f"  {tname} rotated {rname} matches {sname}")

print("\nP tiles vs P subs (checking all rotations):")
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    for sname, spat in p_subs.items():
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if compare(rpat, spat):
                print(f"  {tname} rotated {rname} matches {sname}")

# Also check if any matched tiles need rotation
print("\nChecking all matched tiles for rotation matches:")
matched = [
    ('R2T1', extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 12, 18), 'S2'),
    ('R3T3', extract_b(ascii_lines, 22, 28, 35, 41), 'S4'),
    ('R1T2', extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 22, 28), 'P5'),
    ('R2T2', extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 22, 28), 'P1'),
    ('R3T2', extract_b(ascii_lines, 22, 28, 22, 28), 'P3'),
    ('R3T4', extract_b(ascii_lines, 22, 28, 45, 51), 'P4'),
]
for tname, tpat, sname in matched:
    # Get the target pattern
    if sname.startswith('S'):
        idx = int(sname[1])
        c0 = 14 + (idx-1)*7

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# Unmatched S tiles
s_tiles = {
    'R1T1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 12, 18),
    'R1T3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 35, 41),
    'R2T3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 35, 41),
    'R3T1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 22, 28, 12, 18),
}

# Unmatched S subs
s_subs = {
    'S1': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 14, 20),
    'S3': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 28, 34),
    'S5': extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, 42, 48),
}

# Unmatched P tiles
p_tiles = {
    'R1T4': extract_b(ascii_lines, 4, 10, 45, 51),
    'R2T4': extract_b(ascii_lines, 13, 19, 45, 51),
}

# Unmatched P subs
p_subs = {
    'P2': extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27),
}

print("S tiles vs S subs (checking all rotations):")
for tname, tpat in s_tiles.items():
    for sname, spat in s_subs.items():
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if compare(rpat, spat):
                print(f"  {tname} rotated {rname} matches {sname}")

print("\nP tiles vs P subs (checking all rotations):")
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    for sname, spat in p_subs.items():
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if compare(rpat, spat):
                print(f"  {tname} rotated {rname} matches {sname}")

# Check what this matches
def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# All P tiles
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

# Current P-sub2
p_sub2 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27)

# Check matches
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if compare(rpat, p_sub2):
            print(f"  {tname} rot{rname} matches P-sub2")

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# All P tiles
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

# P-sub4
p_sub4 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 35, 41)
print("P-sub4 B pattern:")
for row in p_sub4:
    print(f"  {row}")
print()

# Check matches
print("Matching P tiles:")
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if compare(rpat, p_sub4):
            print(f"  {tname} rot{rname} matches P-sub4")

# Also check all S tiles against P-sub4

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
initial = history[0].frame
init_lines = initial.ascii.split('\n')
current = current_frame
cur_lines = current.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# All top tiles (from initial state, unchanged)
all_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T1",(4,12)), ("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T3",(4,35)), ("R1T4",(4,45)),
                         ("R2T1",(13,12)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T3",(13,35)), ("R2T4",(13,45)),
                         ("R3T1",(22,12)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T3",(22,35)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    all_tiles[name] = extract_b(init_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

# All original bottom subs
all_subs = {}
for i, c0 in enumerate([14, 21, 28, 35, 42]):
    all_subs[f"S{i+1}"] = extract_b(init_lines, 40, 46, c0, c0+6)
    all_subs[f"P{i+1}"] = extract_b(init_lines, 51, 57, c0, c0+6)

# Determine color of each tile
def get_color(name):
    if name in ['R1T1','R1T3','R2T1','R2T3','R3T1','R3T3']:
        return 'S'
    return 'P'

# Check every tile against every sub with all rotations
print("All tile-sub matches (original state):")
for tname, tpat in all_tiles.items():
    color = get_color(tname)
    for sname, spat in all_subs.items():
        if sname[0] != color:
            continue
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if compare(rpat, spat):
                print(f"  {tname}({color}) rot{rname} -> {sname}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

[TOOL RESULT: python]
All tile-sub matches (original state):

- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# Current P-sub2
p_sub2 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27)
print("P-sub2 B pattern:")
for row in p_sub2:
    print(f"  {row}")

# Check against all P tiles
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if compare(rpat, p_sub2):
            print(f"  {tname} rot{rname} matches P-sub2")

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def compare(p1, p2):
    return all(a==b for a,b in zip(p1,p2))

# Current P-sub2
p_sub2 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27)

# Check against all P tiles with all rotations
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

print("P-sub2 pattern:")
for row in p_sub2:
    print(f"  {row}")

print("\nAll P tile rotations:")
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if compare(rpat, p_sub2):
            print(f"  {tname} rot{rname} matches")
        # Also print each rotation for comparison
    print(f"  {tname}:")
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        print(f"    rot{rname}: {rpat}")

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

# Current P-sub2
p_sub2 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 21, 27)
print("P-sub2 B pattern:")

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# The 5th pattern from history
pat5 = ['.......', '.BBB...', '.B.BBB.', '.B.B.B.', '.B.BBB.', '.BBB...', '.......']

# Check against all P tiles
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

print("Checking 5th pattern against all P tiles:")
for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if all(a==b for a,b in zip(rpat, pat5)):
            print(f"  {tname} rot{rname} matches")

# Also check if it's a combination or transformation
print("\n5th pattern:")
for row in pat5:
    print(f"  {row}")

# Check R1T2 rotations
print("\nR1T2 rotations:")
for rname, rpat in [('0', p_tiles['R1T2']), ('90', rotate90(p_tiles['R1T2'])), ('180', rotate180(p_tiles['R1T2'])), ('270', rotate270(p_tiles['R1T2']))]:

- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. [TOOL CALL: python]

<function=python>
<parameter=code>
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# Get the "?" pattern from history (the 5th DOWN at slot 2)
# history index 8 = after 5th DOWN at slot 2
h = history[8]
lines = h.frame.ascii.split('\n')
pat = extract_b(lines, 51, 57, 21, 27)
print("Pattern ? from history[8]:")
for row in pat:
    print(f"  {row}")

# Check if this is R1T4 rot90 or R1T2 with some rotation
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if all(a==b for a,b in zip(rpat, pat)):
            print(f"  MATCH: {tname} rot{rname}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

<parameter=code>
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# The "?" pattern
pat_q = ['.......', '.BBB...', '.B.BBB.', '.B.B.B.', '.B.BBB.', '.BBB...', '.......']

# Check against ALL 12 tiles
all_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T1",(4,12)), ("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T3",(4,35)), ("R1T4",(4,45)),
                         ("R2T1",(13,12)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T3",(13,35)), ("R2T4",(13,45)),
                         ("R3T1",(22,12)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T3",(22,35)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    all_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

for tname, tpat in all_tiles.items():
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if all(a==b for a,b in zip(rpat, pat_q)):
            print(f"  MATCH: {tname} rot{rname}")

# Also check: maybe it's R1T4 rot90 or R2T4 rot90
print("\nR1T4 rot90:", rotate90(all_tiles['R1T4']))
print("R2T4 rot90:", rotate90(all_tiles['R2T4']))
print("R1T2 rot270:", rotate270(all_tiles['R1T2']))
print("R1T4 rot270:", rotate270(all_tiles['R1T4']))

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.

<parameter=code>
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# Get all tiles
all_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T1",(4,12)), ("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T3",(4,35)), ("R1T4",(4,45)),
                         ("R2T1",(13,12)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T3",(13,35)), ("R2T4",(13,45)),
                         ("R3T1",(22,12)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T3",(22,35)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    all_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

# P4 original pattern
p4 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 35, 41)
print("P4 current B pattern:")
for row in p4:
    print(f"  {row}")
print()

# Check R1T4, R2T4, R3T3 against P4
for tname in ['R1T4', 'R2T4', 'R3T3']:
    tpat = all_tiles[tname]
    print(f"{tname}:")
    for row in tpat:
        print(f"  {row}")
    rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
    for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
        if all(a==b for a,b in zip(rpat, p4)):
            print(f"  MATCH: rot{rname}")
    print()
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# S panel slots
s_slots = {}
for name, c0 in [("S1",14), ("S2",21), ("S3",28), ("S4",35), ("S5",42)]:
    s_slots[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, 40, 46, c0, c0+6)

# S tiles
s_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T1",(4,12)), ("R1T3",(4,35)), ("R2T1",(13,12)), ("R2T3",(13,35)), ("R3T1",(22,12)), ("R3T3",(22,35))]:
    s_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

# Check each S slot against all S tiles
print("S panel matching:")
for sname, spat in s_slots.items():
    matches = []
    for tname, tpat in s_tiles.items():
        rotations = {'0': tpat, '90': rotate90(tpat), '180': rotate180(tpat), '270': rotate270(tpat)}
        for rname, rpat in rotations.items():
            if all(a==b for a,b in zip(rpat, spat)):
                matches.append(f"{tname} rot{rname}")
    print(f"  {sname}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

# Also check against P tiles
p_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T2",(4,22)), ("R1T4",(4,45)), ("R2T2",(13,22)), ("R2T4",(13,45)), ("R3T2",(22,22)), ("R3T4",(22,45))]:
    p_tiles[name] = extract_b(ascii_lines, r0, r0+6, c0, c0+6)

for sname, spat in s_slots.items():
    matches = []
    for tname, tpat in p_tiles.items():

ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def extract_b(lines, r0, r1, c0, c1):
    return [''.join('B' if ch=='B' else '.' for ch in lines[r][c0:c1+1]) for r in range(r0, r1+1)]

def rotate90(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[n-1-c][r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

def rotate180(pat):
    return [row[::-1] for row in pat[::-1]]

def rotate270(pat):
    n = len(pat)
    return [''.join(pat[c][n-1-r] for c in range(n)) for r in range(n)]

# P4 original pattern
p4 = extract_b(ascii_lines, 51, 57, 35, 41)
print("P4 pattern:")
for row in p4:
    print(f"  {row}")
print()

# S tiles
s_tiles = {}
for name, (r0, c0) in [("R1T1",(4,12)), ("R1T3",(4,35)), ("R2T1",(13,12)), ("R2T3",(13,35)), ("R3T1",(22,12)), ("R3T3",(22,35))]:
